- Cys as nucleophile (attacks the substrate)

- The Cys is typically in the N-terminal region
- The His is typically further in the sequence
- The Glu is also in the sequence

Autophosphorylation**: RTKs are activated by ligand-induced dimerization followed by trans-autophosphorylation on tyrosine residues. This is the hallmark PTM of RTKs — the kinase modifies *itself*.<br>- **B. Proteolytic cleavage**: Some RTKs undergo ectodomain shedding, but this is not the defining signaling-associated PTM.<br><br>**Step 3: Key question distinction**<br>The question specifically asks about a modification of &quot;this signaling protein itself, rather than merely describing a role it might have in modifying another protein.&quot; This phrasing contrasts the kinase&#x27;s catalytic function (phosphorylating other proteins) with what happens *to* the kinase itself. **Autophosphorylation** is precisely this — the kinase phosphorylates itself, which is the defining activation mechanism unique to receptor tyrosine kinases.<br><br>2. answer: A</td>

Now, distinguishing between options:

- Option B: Phosphorylates uridine to UMP → This would be uridine kinase (much larger enzyme, ~480 aa in humans)
- Option C: Phosphorylates deoxycytidine to dCMP → This would be deoxycytidine kinase (~250 aa)
- Option D: Deaminates free cytosine to uracil → This would be cytosine deaminase

The question asks us to &quot;compare the substrate-to-product changes implied by the alternatives rather than relying on shared pathway context alone.&quot;

Options A and D both involve deamination (removing an amino group), converting cytosine/cytidine to uracil/uridine. The difference is:
- A acts on nucleosides (cytidine and deoxycytidine) → uridine and deoxyuridine
- D acts on free bases (cytosine) → uracil

Options B and C involve phosphorylation (adding a phosphate group).

Given the protein&#x27;s characteristics:
- Small size (~150 aa) - consistent with cytidine deaminase
- Zinc-binding motifs with cysteines - characteristic of cytidine deaminase
- Cytidine deaminase can act on both cytidine and deoxycytidine

This protein appears to be a cytidine deaminase (CDA), which catalyzes the deamination of cytidine and 2&#x27;-deoxycytidine to form uridine and 2&#x27;-deoxyuridine, respectively. This is consistent with option A.

Cytosine deaminase (option D) is typically a different enzyme with different sequence characteristics (often larger, and found in different organisms like E. coli). The zinc-binding site architecture in this sequence is more consistent with a cytidine (nucleoside) deaminase rather than a cytosine (base) deaminase.

My answer is A.
